ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e3296	Federal Minister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e3312	Ladies and gentlemen, we have already set up a working group some time ago, where the Association of Austrian Private Senders, the ORF, the Association of Austrian Newspapers and other representatives of affected groups were invited to consider what national possibilities – including legal ones – Austria has in this context and which areas can simply and simply be regulated at European level – because there is responsibility there. For example, it is a question of modern copyright, of a right to benefit, of a right of competition, of reducing the partly dominant position of certain companies and companies that you have listed. We are also discussing at European level, there are several initiatives there. What we have also considered with these experts who have sent us these different organizations, was whether we may also be able to put in place measures that lead to more equality in tax terms – the word advertising that is not currently extended to digital advertising, i.e. whether it could make sense whether there are technical possibilities or what technical possibilities to produce “weapons” equality more – under quotation marks. We are in intensive discussions with the various actors in order to find a meaningful common solution. I have already said here – I think the survey was 2009 – that it is essentially about that we, or the various participants in the Austrian media market, do not fight in different ways, because the others are simply and simply stronger.
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e3336	Additional question? Please, Mr Cap.
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e3354	As you have just referred to the European Union: Are there more specific projects, and what is the significance of the local media market or the central concerns of Austria?
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e3370	Federal Minister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e3385	Yes, there are several initiatives at European level. The European Commission is currently working on a revision of the Audiovisual Media Services Directive, i.e. the basis for media legislation in each Member State. There should actually be a design in the summer, there are of course also our employees in the Federal Chancellery in regular discussions. We also expect proposals for the re-regulation of European copyright, because we have seen that many things are now extremely cross-border, of course, through the Internet, and there are certain points that are relatively difficult for Austria in particular if this is not maintained. The keyword is, for example, geoblocking. And the European Commission has also carried out a consultation on Internet platforms last autumn, which will continue to work on.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e3424	Minister! ==References====External links== In June 2015, in the course of the reform dialogue on administrative simplification, a number of measures were adopted to reduce red tape and to improve the efficiency and proximity of citizens in the administration, which will be implemented as soon as possible into a collective law. One area is also the widespread abolition of the cumulation principle in administrative criminal law, since it often leads to unwarranted levels of punishment. To this end, there are draft opinions from well-known experts. My question is: 182/M “When will you submit to Parliament a draft law to prevent, among other things, unwarranted burdens by the cumulation principle in administrative criminal law in the future in line with judicial criminal law?”
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e3448	Federal Minister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e3463	So when exactly I present the draft, I can't tell you yet. We have agreed in the coalition that, in addition to this reform dialogue on administrative simplification mentioned by you, we are using an interministerial working group, which deals specifically only with the topic of revision of administrative criminal law, including the principle of cumulation. At the beginning of this month, we asked the main departments concerned to make each expert named – this is the Federal Chancellery, the Ministry of Economic Affairs, the Federal Ministry for Labour, Social Affairs and Consumer Protection, the BMVIT, the BMF, because it is logically also about the question of revenue, about the BMI and the BMLFUW, that is the agricultural resort, and also about the BMJ. We will also have to include the countries because, in part, administrative penalties will flow into the Landesbanken, and we will also invite the social partners, at least the Chamber of Commerce and the Chamber of Workers, to do so. It is about administrative penalties or the discussion we are now discussing, on the one hand, about the question of cumulation, on the other, of the purpose of these penalties which we are talking about. It is a difference whether it is about prohibiting cell phone calls or whether it is about measures which, for example, should restrict wage and social dumping. The working group will thus begin its work as soon as the experts are nominated, and as soon as one has consensus on a draft law, we will present it.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e3542	The question in the case of criminal law, whether the criminal law or administrative criminal law regulations are, is always driving towards a particular, socially desired behavior. The second principle is that we always have the means to use them. We do not start with prison sentences either, but in part we start with disversion, in part with teaching, with admonition and in part in the future with fines. Whether we need to further anchor the principle of “consultation instead of punishment” or whether the current administrative criminal law regulations are sufficient, this group will also discuss this. So I cannot tell you any results now – and would not find that fair to the participants of the working group, if I were to tell you a final result.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e6324	Mr. Federal Minister, on a very different subject. My question is: 187/M "One of the most up-to-date challenges in the field of fundamental rights is the redesign of data protection in Europe through the EU General Data Protection Regulation. – From today’s point of view, how do you plan their implementation into national law?”
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e6345	Please, Minister of State.
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e6361	The EU's data protection package, consisting of the General Data Protection Regulation and the Privacy Directive on Justice and Home Affairs, is expected to be published on 21 March 2014. It was decided by the Council in April and, of course, then discussed and decided in plenary session. The aim is to publish the package by June 2016 in the Official Journal. We then have two Areas: One is the basic regulation. This is to be applied immediately, but there is a significant need for adaptation in national data protection law, in particular in the DSG 2000. The second area is the GDPR, i.e. the basic regulation, which also needs national design, especially because there are some opening clauses and so on. The deadline is two years, so this must be implemented internationally by June 2018 at the latest. What we are doing is that, when it enters into force, we are developing a ministerial draft, including the relevant actors in the form of an assessment, but also in advance, and trying to use the scope that the EU approves of, to do what we have always said and wherever we have all consensus, namely to keep the level of data protection in Austria as high as possible.

This list could, I think, continue to be infinitely far. Unfortunately, it is also continuing in the cultural field. As has been said, we are of course also opposed to the illegal trade in cultural goods. But I think that by this law one tries to solve the problem primarily at the expense of the economic operators. So if this were to be the solution to the problem, I'd say: Let us look at the problem again and look for another solution! So illegal cultural trade: no, of course not! ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e7201	Next speaker: Mrs Dr. Karl. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e7223	Madam President. Mr. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! The illegal movement and illegal trade in cultural goods endanger cultural heritage not only in Europe but in all parts of the world. What dimensions this assumes have already been carried out by my predecessor. The European Union and UNESCO have therefore created legal instruments to counter this effectively. At EU level, the current Directive has been replaced by a new directive on the return of cultural goods unlawfully transferred from the territory of a Member State. This Directive has created within the EU a system for the protection of cultural goods from their illegal exports. Cooperation mechanisms between national authorities as well as judicial procedures for the return of unlawfully spent cultural goods are provided for. However, the Directive to be transposed with this Government template applies only to cultural goods exported between Member States of the EU. The 1970 UNESCO Convention is therefore of relevance to the international level. It creates similar rules to the EU Directive and now has more than 120 Contracting States. Among these Contracting States are particularly important countries such as the USA, the United Kingdom, France, Switzerland, Germany and the Netherlands. Austria was Thus, before accession to this Convention, it was in an increasingly isolated position and was also accused of providing a forum for the illicit trade in cultural goods. The UNESCO Convention requires, inter alia, preventive measures such as the introduction and monitoring of export licences for cultural goods, the preparation of inventory, continuous education measures and various criminal sanctions. These obligations are already being fulfilled by Austria in particular through the Monument Protection Act and the activity of the Federal Monumental Office. It is to be welcomed that the present government proposal also sets out measures to protect cultural property against its illegal exports and to combat illegal trade in cultural goods. Thank you.

Of course, exports are also prohibited. And for the museums there are appropriate rules that they should not or should not acquire unlawfully exported cultural goods, stolen cultural goods. It is also common in our legal order whether or not drugs or other prohibitions are being dealt with. That is why I did not understand the criticism of Mrs Weigerstorfer, if one is to say that trade in raw materials, the business of destroyed cultural goods is prohibited. We also discussed it with the European Council of Ministers for Culture at the end of last year. That this was also one of the sources of income of the IS, we know simply and simply, and therefore it is only about trade, only about the prohibition of making money by avoiding the destruction of cultural goods in order to be executed. The fact that they are destroyed for totally irrational reasons is difficult to prevent in war zones, but that they are destroyed or stolen to trade with them, we must stop. That's what we're doing with this law, and that's just about trade. Furthermore, of course we also discussed it with the art trade and did not refuse the conversation. Thank you very much for agreeing to the implementation of a commitment we received 46 years ago, I believe, and thus strengthening the protection of cultural goods. – Thank you very much.

[[The President-in-Office of the Council shall take over the Presidency.]] The loss of these cultural goods is an immeasurable one. The Cultural Goods Return Act regulates return claims by means of a secure legal basis. New to this law is that it also applies to countries which are not members of the European Union. This also increases the obligations of care in the commercial trade in art. In future, records must be kept for 30 years. Thus, we take into account the EU Directive and the UNESCO Convention, which sets binding standards worldwide, which was introduced in 2014. We've already heard it, it's about uniform standards. The law improves cooperation, but it also improves the communication of the authorities through the relevant internal market information system. And it also gives the opportunity to make intergovernmental agreements to protect the cultural heritage. My previous speakers have already mentioned it, and in recent years, terrorist organisations with illegal cultural trade have made some money to finance their activities. It is important, therefore, that the base be withdrawn from it and that it is removed, because where there is no market and the sales opportunities are low, the merit is naturally unattractive, and then the matter is no longer interesting. And of course this should be the goal: to restrict illegal trade!

ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e9142	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! What happens if Austria concludes a State Treaty which is approved here in Parliament, and the Constitutional Court then finds that the resulting laws or the law on this State Treaty are unconstitutional? What happens then? – This is a completely unclear state. We then have a constitutional crisis, and in reality no one knows how to deal with it, because either the government behaves unconstitutional or it has to strive to repeal this state treaty. You see, if that happens, that would be a completely unsatisfactory situation, and that may be the case at any time: We recall the ESM, the European Stability Mechanism, where it was very questionable whether it was constitutional, but a state treaty was concluded and subsequently it was examined by the Constitutional Court. Fortunately – from the perspective of the described situation – it was found to be constitutional. What about TTIP? – TTIP would also be a law-changing state contract. We will ultimately have to decide TTIP inside here. There is great criticism of this, and there are also many questions as to whether TTIP – at least in parts – is unconstitutional. What's going to happen? – The train will follow in the direction of the Constitutional Court. Perhaps the Constitutional Court finds that parts of it are unconstitutional. Then what are we doing? Will post-negotiation then be conducted, or will the government or authorities acting under this Treaty be unconstitutional? You see, it is important – and this is the request made by the FPÖ – that before they are decided here, state agreements can be examined for their constitutionality. There are many supporters for this. As early as 2012, this discussion was held, at the time the President of the Constitutional Court of Holzinger welcomed this very much and said that it would be good. The colleague Cap, who was present here, also spoke out in 2012 and welcomed this very much. President Fischer has welcomed it very much. – These are people who also have to do with it, who know what it means when such a situation occurs. We have therefore already made a specific request in 2014 years ago, which has always been postponed. Now we are back in such a situation – as said, TTIP is at the doorstep – but what is happening? It is only applied: Well, we have to examine this in detail now! That is why the government parties have now tabled a motion for a resolution, which says: We need to consider how this can systematically be brought into Austrian law and whether we can do comparative studies with other countries. The is the reaction after three or four years of discussion – four years after this problem was clearly identified. Shortly before it may occur again – namely at TTIP – an opinion will now be obtained. I know that a counter-argument is that our request represents a certain number of Members who can request such an examination of a state contract. This would be systemic, because the number doesn't match. Yes, then, for example, we change this number to a third, which would be the same quota as it would be necessary to have a law examined! We are immediately in favour of it, essentially, a regulation is being adopted here which allows for this examination. It comes, I know, also the argument: Well, we don't want the Constitutional Court to make legislative audits before something is decided here. – Completely right, but that is something else. A law binds us internally, we can do so at any time; but a state treaty binds us outside, and that is the decisive difference. The argument that we do not want legislative audits by the Constitutional Court before the laws are passed – which I fully share – therefore does not apply here. However, the examination of the state agreements is important, so I can only appeal once again, not to push it to the long-term bank and to make a motion for a resolution after four years of discussion, to get an opinion suddenly and to delay it again, by the way, without any deadline, but to follow our argument, which is simple and simple – I know that there are many who support it in essence here – and to make this change to the Constitution.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e9558	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! Mr Stefan has put forward some good arguments as to why it is quite useful to provide for a preliminary examination of state agreements by the Constitutional Court of Justice. [[Abg. Stefan: As always!]] However, he also argued that there are also counter-arguments, especially from systematic considerations. I, too, consider this system to be problematic from a systematic point of view and see some issues that are still open and which must therefore be discussed even further. Of course, scientific expertise is a good basis for this. Where do I see problems from a systematic point of view? I would like to side by side with the aspect that has already been raised, how many Members can put forward a request, and I would like to address another aspect. The Constitutional Court has, among other things, the task of examining state agreements for their legality. Like the decisions on the illegality of laws or the lawfulness of regulations, the decision on the illegality of state contracts is always only taken in the form of an audit of standards. A preliminary examination of state agreements would mean a deviation from this principle and would be problematic against the background of the separation of violence. Legislative is not the task of the Constitutional Court, but of Parliament and should remain here. The Constitutional Court should not replace the democratically legitimate legislator and thus undermine Parliament's legislative competence. One more aspect is added, ladies and gentlemen, and underlines the systemicity of this proposal: Verification of standards by the Constitutional Court shall be carried out in a specific case. The Constitutional Court does not have the competence to carry out a general legal review. To this end, there is the Constitutional Service and the International Law Bureau. The Constitutional Service, established in the Federal Chancellery, has extensive expertise. It assesses all draft laws and regulations from the Federal Ministries for their compatibility with constitutional law. Of course, the competence of the International Law Bureau can also be used. If the Constitutional Court is now appointed as a legal expert in the parliamentary process, the question also arises which consequence has a prior general legal review for subsequent proceedings for the examination of state contracts for their legality. Imagine, a state contract is issued by the Constitutional Court in advance of a so-called persil license. Can this State Treaty, in the consequence, be subject to further verification of the legality of the matter? If so, what chance do you have in such a procedure? That means: Is it really realistic that the Constitutional Court is still derogating from its prejudicial decision? So you see, ladies and gentlemen, There are many questions, many questions are open. It is therefore to be welcomed that an opinion on this issue will be obtained. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e11088	Mr President. Federal Minister! High house! Dear fellow citizens and fellow citizens! On the current occasion, I would like to introduce the subject of the UK EU referendum into the debate – to be found on page 6 of the current report of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. I am afraid that the agreement reached at the last Council meeting on the re-establishment of Great Britain's relationship with the EU will have a profound and lasting negative impact on the Union. First, it should be noted that the agreement fulfils virtually all British claims, whereas the other 27 countries have not agreed to make a single counterclaim. One could have taken the opportunity to explore the long list of special rules and exceptions that Great Britain has accumulated, perhaps starting with the British discount, which Prime Minister Thatcher had negotiated in 1984, when Britain was still the second poorest country in the EEC, and today, 30 years later, is calling on the UK to continue, although it has a per capita income higher than in Italy and France. No, instead, the Council has almost panicily concluded the negotiations within three months and without a defense, has fulfilled every call for Cameron to strengthen it internally and in the hope of averting the Brexit almost at all costs. The initial approach to initiating a reform of the Union has remained entirely on the road. This is a pity, because much of what the British criticise in the EU is actually not working properly. The opportunity to reform the institutions in the sense of greater subsidiarity to strengthen democratic legitimacy, to bring the EU closer to the citizens, to improve the decision-making process has been denied. Above all, however, the agreement has exceeded some red lines. On the one hand, the reduction of certain social benefits for EU citizens undermines the principle of free movement of persons, and it is therefore shaken by a Union pillar. On the other hand, national parliaments should now have the opportunity to block EU legislation by means of the so-called Red Card. So instead of expanding the competences of the EU Parliament, let us go back one step. Great Britain has, of course, never made a significant contribution to the deepening of the Union, but this time Britain is making a cold-blooded return. Haider: Thank God!]] Welcome to UKIP! Of course, the United Kingdom has never shared the objective of a political project, and 43 years after accession, at best, the Kingdom sees a kind of turbo-charged free trade zone in the EU. Even a alleged advocate of the EU, Prime Minister Cameron, clearly admits in relation to the ever closer Union: ‘The confession (...) shall no longer apply to the United Kingdom. We don't believe that. We're not behind it. We have a different vision of Europe." Europe is, of course, primarily a political project. That's always been it from the beginning. At the time of the establishment of the ECSC in 1951, the objective was to maintain peace within Europe. And today the European Union is still a political agreement project. It was it in the reception of the satellite countries of the former Soviet Union, it is today in the integration of the Balkan countries, it could be for Ukraine tomorrow. And people in these states see in the European Union not only a symbol of prosperity, but also the epitome of freedom, democracy, stability and rule of law. The British are also reminded that every generation must re-establish peace. This is what the wars in the former Yugoslavia have brought to our attention, and today we are showing the Russian aggression in Ukraine and also the Syria conflict. Cameron is therefore mistaken if he claims that today the main purpose of the EU is another, namely “no longer to bring peace, but to secure prosperity”. That, ladies and gentlemen, Mr Federal Minister, is a small-scale, forward-looking view of the European Union. It was not for the British people to have this view. But we do not share them, we have higher ambitions for Europe and for its peoples. Therefore, it would be a capital mistake to suspend Article 1 of the EU Treaty for Great Britain and to establish that individual Member States should not only move forward at different speeds – which makes sense – but can also pursue diametrically opposed objectives – which is absurd and potentially destructive. No matter what the outcome of the referendum on 23 May. It will be June, it will not be good, because even in the case of a UK stay in the EU, the Council's decision has permanently damaged the Union. The British will not learn to love the Union, they will continue to reject further progress in the future, torpedo a common foreign and defence policy and strengthen national tendencies. Thus, I wonder if a terror with no end to Great Britain is preferable to an end with terror without the Kingdom – thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e12440	We're now on the 8th. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. Mr Gahr has spoken on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats). - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e12467	Mr President. Federal Minister! (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) The citizens' initiative on double citizenship for South Tyrol was supported by more than 22,000 people, brought in and handed over directly here in Parliament. Since 2011 – and this has been a long period of time – has been discussed intensively. There was an intensive political exchange in the Committee on Petitions and Citizens' Initiatives, in the subcommittee of South Tyrol, but also in the Committee on Foreign Policy. There was a hearing in which the various positions were thoroughly examined and advised. Of course, there were also political discussions with South Tyrol at all political levels – at the level of the country and also at the bilateral level. All in all, I believe that this citizens' initiative has brought many emotions, but, I believe, it has been thoroughly discussed. I therefore believe that we have done everything we could do here. The current situation and situation shows that at present the citizens' initiative's desire for dual citizenship cannot be met: There is simply no political consensus and no political majority, we must take note of this reality. There are advocates in South Tyrol, but also opponents who critically see and also reject this double citizenship. There are also people in Austria who are positive about this double citizenship, because it also creates a connection with the fatherland of Austria, but there are also critics who warn of the consequences. All in all, I think it is quite clear: This desire from South Tyrol remains, and there will continue to be discussions. It is our honest effort and our commitment that, despite this situation, we make a clear confession to South Tyrol and take the concerns of the South Tyroleans seriously. As in the past, priority has been given to the protection function of 1946: The Paris Treaty has so far been an irrevocable basis and must continue to do so in the future. The negotiations currently under way on constitutional reform in Italy have the potential to shift skills. Despite the negotiated safeguard clause, no deterioration in the autonomous provinces and regions in South Tyrol is to occur as a result of the constitutional reform, but rather a dynamisation clause should bring new competences from the state to the regions. With this motion for a resolution on the protection function, we want to give ourselves a clear mandate for the future in the Austrian Parliament. It is an independent and positive development of South Tyrol and a strong, active Europe region of Tyrol. Thank you.

The fact that we are important to South Tyrol as well as to South Tyrol is a fact, and that the country of South Tyrol can rely a hundred percent on Austria in case of need, is a fact that we are interested in and closely accompany the development of autonomy. She's afraid to go to sleep and close her eyes. This report clearly documents – and he makes this quite clear – that only traveling women with and without children, pregnant or breast-feeding, young women, unaccompanied children, early married women and already babies in poor women are particularly vulnerable, and therefore they need more coordinated and efficient protection from all of us. ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e17341	Mr President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! In a traditional understanding of the relationship between police and human rights a reduced picture appears very often: Human rights, in the sense of their historical origin, are very often regarded as civil and political rights and as defence rights against hand-in-hand The Commission's proposal for a Council Directive on the approximation of the laws, regulations and administrative provisions of the Member States relating to the approximation of the On the other hand, police work is very often understood in the public debate on the issue of combating crime in the sense of investigating and transferring criminal persons. Consequently, defence rights against state intervention should be understood as an obstacle to efficient and effective police work. This creates a bipolar position: On the one hand, the police are in the interest of security with the right to enforce legal powers; Restrictions in this enforcement are often perceived as an obstacle. For a very long time, this led to a negative connotation in the relationship between police and human rights. On the other hand, there is a civil society that has understood the activities of the police from the perspective of defence rights against the action of the state. The modern development of police work, which also pursues Austria, suggests overcoming this bipolar position. Human rights are therefore no longer defined as a restriction of police work, but as their essential basis and objective of police work. Human rights as guiding principles of constitutional law define comprehensive guarantees and intentions for the functioning of the rule of law. The police, therefore, must not be passive about the implementation of human rights, but must at times be actively involved in the protection of human rights. The project “Police.Macht.Menchen.Rights” has very well prepared the ground for police work in Austria. Through the active use of civil society representatives, dialogue platforms have been implemented in which various issues could be discussed and discussed in peace and in a de-escaling framework – and the whole at a glance. These are the prerequisites for new development perspectives and perspectives that we are dealing with today. The aim is to create an open and appreciative culture of conversation between the police and society and to ensure that the greatest possible confidence in the security and work of the police is guaranteed for the population. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen, at this point, my greatest thanks and respect for all men and women in uniform, who, as part of the police work for the security of this country, do quite great work. Thank you very much. They really do great work! [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ.]] Speaking great work: In one of my previous speeches to the Foreign Minister, I thanked him for his clear attitude in the situation of refugees, which is very demanding for us. These also have to be very often social workers, psychologists and so on, because our societies have become more complex. In this situation, we should support the police to the best of their knowledge and conscience and to the best of their strengths, so that they can handle and handle the more difficult task that is probably not easier, but rather more difficult, in the next few years. This project police.Macht.People.Rights in the Ministry of the Interior have expired in 2015, so it is understandable and also good that colleague Kirchgatterer and colleague Pfurtscheller made this request. We will also agree to the request because it is only possible to agree to it because we have to support the police in dealing with their task. In this context, I would like to thank all the police officers in the country very much, aware of the fact that human rights do not apply to them. For that small minority in the police, which unfortunately does not see it that way – or not yet – we need our clear stand and support so that we can realize this saying that the police are the country's largest human rights organisation. - Thank you many times.

These principles include, in particular, the entire organisation, the entire area of personnel selection, including daily operational work, behaviour and behaviour towards citizens and also the entire area of employee management. I see these orientations as very big milestones, because they have been developed by both the headquarters of the BMI and the police, and above all in close cooperation with the NGOs, with which civil society has been involved. What is the focus of the citizens’ initiative “Free School Election 2.0”? – With the idea that non-confessional private schools are treated the same as public schools and religious schools. And this is the idea, in turn, that funds are actually spent not on individual objects, but on students. This means that a private school, which does not require school fees, receives the same expenses from the state as a public school. This is the central concern of the citizens' initiative. When did she present this for the first time? – In 2011, there was a citizens’ initiative, then still handed over to the now unfortunately dead Madam President Prammer, with more than 20,000 signatures. This came to the Committee on Petitions, where the opinions received. Then it went to the teaching committee, from there it went to the teaching subcommittee, and there it died somewhere. This initiative, however, – which unfortunately cannot be demanded of everyone – has so much seat meat and so much strength that it does not give up. Further initiatives have then been launched and the whole thing has been brought back to Parliament. And what is happening now is totally striking for how we work in our committees. We have now received the opinions of the teaching institute. The private school law was passed in 1962, since private schools were very small, and the problems that private schools are facing today were still unknown. If you want to change this private school law – and the initiative calls for it – the corresponding department answers, a change is not necessary, because the law is already in operation, runs for religious private schools and so on; but the department does not respond at all to the actual demand of the citizens' initiative and then – and this is, of course, the upper-gag – refers to federalism, i.e. the lack of partial competence in the compulsory schools. Only because this lack of partial competence is not available in compulsory schools has this subject obviously not been treated more seriously in 2011. What happens in the teaching committee now? – I would like to be a pessimist from the optimist to the pessimist. Since there is very little happening in the field of education, it is likely to land again in the Subcommittee of the Teaching Committee – we now have a regular legislative period until 2018 – where it will be postponed regularly. At the end or in the meantime, it will be noted at some point, and the initiator and initiators will again receive a half-paged letter: Dear initiator, dear initiator, we have taken note of your concern. It'll be ten years soon. And we need to work on it, that it won't happen in the future.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e19371	Mr President. (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) I would like to address the 60 citizens’ initiative, “Equal rights for chronically ill children”. This citizens' initiative calls on the National Council to adopt legislative measures to ensure equal rights for chronically ill children throughout Austria. There are currently about 190 000 children and adolescents affected by chronic diseases and a legislative initiative is intended to ensure that these children and young people do not suffer as usual discrimination at school or at events. That's the initiative. This has been the subject of the Committee on Petitions and has received a total of four opinions: from the Federal Ministry of Health, the Federal Ministry of Education and Women, the Federal Ministry for Labour, Social Affairs and Consumer Protection and also from the Public Prosecutor's Office. There are actually very good, detailed opinions from all these ministries and the People's Procuratorate. The Federal Ministry of Health, in its opinion, focuses mainly on the law in force, namely that in the medical law it is regulated that in the schools through which, if they are present, it is a school doctor and a school doctor. ed to provide chronically ill children and adolescents. If that is not the case, then – and the BMG is also involved – the delegation right created with the 2003 medical novel, according to which teachers can be seen as laymen and can also provide chronically ill children in schools. Thus, from the BMG's point of view, the citizens' initiative would actually be implemented. On the one hand, there should be an information campaign for teachers so that they are aware of this task, on the other hand However, the BMG also wants to ensure that the voluntaryity set out in the Medical Law remains a voluntaryity. It should not be so that this voluntaryity – I say now – becomes an automatic duty, because educators are primarily teachers and not doctors or nurses. Therefore, the BMG considers that the Medical Law actually represents this requirement. We should also inform the citizens' initiative – as Mr Pock said – and, of course, look at the fact that a awareness-raising measure actually ensures that chronically ill children and young people in schools do not experience discrimination.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e19570	The next speaker is Mr Magistrate. Steinhauser. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e19592	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to comment on a citizens' initiative, which is particularly important to me, which is the citizens' initiative to strengthen the rights of those affected by accidents. When we talk about accident victims, we talk about people who have been hit hard by fate – usually it's car accidents, but it doesn't always have to be car accidents – people who are usually seriously injured and then have to wage an endless fight for damages and accident pensions. And the problem is, it's a fight against Goliath: on the one hand, an accident-damaged person who has to take a hard blow is usually not financially strong, and on the other hand, an insurance that is financially strong and employs the best lawyers to prevent the claim for damages. The following problem then arises: In fact, there are only two ways for those concerned either to compare quickly – that is, of course the goal of insurance, since this comparison for insurance is usually relatively cheap – or to engage in an endless tough fight in court, which is hard to win and usually ends with the fact that those concerned sometimes collapse financially. This imbalance is actually unbearable and is reinforced by a special misunderstanding in Austrian law, which the petition has also taken into consideration here, namely the problem – and we are increasingly experiencing this in court – that opinions, medical opinions in this case, decide on the outcome of the procedure. As a rule, evidence issues play a subordinate role in such procedures, legal issues play a subordinate role. As a rule, there are several medical experts who write an opinion, and in the end, the judge decides on the basis of this opinion. And the specific problem in Austria is that there is no effective quality control of these opinions and a large part of these opinions is not the state of science. That is why it is a mistake because it results in the people concerned not having a chance to get to their right due to this abuse. The petition has just begun, and the Ministry of Justice has responded in a way that is completely incomprehensible, as the Ministry of Justice needs to be clear that this quality control does not work. The Ministry of Justice, in its opinion to the Committee on Petitions, stated that quality control was primarily the responsibility of the parties themselves and the courts and, subsequently, the President of the Regional Court. - That's not how it works! In general, judges can only very, very difficult to estimate whether these opinions have been carried out according to the state of science, for this requires medical knowledge. They may be able to examine whether this is final, whether everything has been reviewed, but whether it corresponds to the scientific state, this can be very difficult for a court to judge. The parties have a harder time, because what do the parties do when they say: Wait, I've had a serious accident, sit in the wheelchair since then, but is that supposed to be not accidental? – Then they order a private property. tr. The private expert shows the difference. And what does the court do? – The court says: Well, private experts, these are ordered utility opinions! – In other words, the party has no chance of fulfilling in reality this quality control which the Ministry of Justice attributes. People stay on the track. Therefore, it would be the central task to eliminate this abuse in our rule of law, that there is an independent quality control of these opinions, because otherwise we are faced with the situation that, while we are laying down precise rules of procedure, we define exactly what is to be judged, but that it is not determined what a court opinion has to look like. And this is a situation that is facing those who are particularly affected in this difficult situation which cannot be dealt with. And the result is clear: At some point, all give up, are rightly frustrated and disappointed by life and feel abandoned by the rule of law and by this republic. The response of the Ministry of Justice is a problem that I think is unacceptable, as it simply ignores that we have a serious problem in a very specific segment of the rule of law. That is why I would have liked this petition to be assigned to the Justice Committee, so that we can discuss it with the Minister for Justice. I am convinced that if you talk to Minister of Justice Brandstetter, he will recognize relatively quickly – even because of his scientific know-how as a criminal law professor – where the problems lie, and perhaps even then would be willing to think about it. By wiping this away, we are trying to save the debate. We are going to bring this to the Justice Committee anyway, but it would have been an important signal to those concerned that they are taken seriously and that this problem is being discussed in the end, that they are at the end of their thinking and that they have been eliminated. This opportunity has been given, and then you must not be surprised if citizens of Parliament are also disappointed because they do not feel taken seriously in their core affairs. And the key issue is simply the opportunity to have a fair trial and to come to his right after a serious accident. We will stick to it, but in fact it would have been appropriate if Parliament had also assigned this matter to the judicial committee so that we could discuss it there. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-03-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00119_d7e19677	Mr President. I would like to join with the previous speaker in his speech and speak on the same citizens' initiative, on the rights of victims of accidents. Of course, it depends very much on the quality of the experts and, accordingly, on the opinions. For many victims, it is very essential that the quality of such an assessment is whether it is correct in terms of content, whether or not opinions are wrongfully detrimental to the victims concerned. This can have an absolute existential or real impact on the social and economic situation of victims of accidents. [[President Hofer shall take over the chairmanship.]] This citizens' initiative calls, among other things, for the improvement of training, certification and review of experts. It is true that the Minister of Justice has replied in a very comprehensive way in his reply to the question as to what the status quo looks like. I still think that, apart from the status quo, there is really a lot to do and a lot to move on and develop. The Commission's proposal for a Council Directive on the It is a matter of urgency, of perceptibility, of completeness of advice that makes sense. This also applies to sanctions where opinions are party. In many cases, training of judges and judges would be more than necessary, just as the training of the experts themselves would be necessary. One should try to get to the state of modern science and to qualify judges as far as possible, at least in their special areas, provided that they can at all develop them to be at the level of science in order to assess whether opinions are meaningful and qualitatively good or not. And I think that we should also be on the sides of the weaker ones and look at this issue in detail in the Committee on Justice, where it seems that the Greens are still going to introduce it. Thank you.
